SPY 778.57 +0.60% QQQ 751.38 +0.51% BTC/USD 83,705.27 +0.94% DIA 516.21 +0.89% GLD 384.50 +1.55% TLT 77.97 +0.12%
Dow Jones, S&P 500, Nasdaq 100 Outlook: Top Stock Market News This Week
The Dow Jones , S&P 500 and Nasdaq 100 have held steady in recent weeks as investors have focused on rising US bond yields and the escalating situation in the Middle East. Their resilience will be tested in the coming weeks as major US companies report earnings and key economic data is released.
Dow Jones, S&P 500, and Nasdaq 100 to React to Earnings Season
The main catalyst for the US stock market this week will be the upcoming earnings season , which will kick off on Tuesday. Top US banks, including popular names like Goldman Sachs, Morgan Stanley and JPMorgan, will release their numbers. Other non-bank companies that will release their numbers are Johnson & Johnson, UnitedHealth Group and Kinder Morgan.
Analysts are optimistic about this earnings season. FactSet (NYSE: FDS ) data shows that the estimate among analysts is that earnings growth will be 29.5%. Historically, the real earnings growth tends to be 7% above the estimate. As such, there is a likelihood that the growth will be over 40%.
The earnings growth comes at a time when American stocks are trading at discount levels. For example, the forward price-to-earnings ratio for the S&P 500 Index is 19.1, lower than the five and ten-year averages.
Why Micron Stock is Struggling Despite Revenue Growth and a Low Valuation
Micron stock price has remained inside a narrow range in the past few days despite its strong revenue growth and cheap valuation
The stock market will also react to the upcoming US consumer price index (CPI) report on Wednesday. Economists expect the data to show that the headline and core CPI numbers rose last month as gasoline and diesel prices jumped.
The average estimate among analysts is that the headline CPI rose from 3.4% in August to 3.6% in September. Core inflation, which excludes the volatile food and energy prices, is expected to come in at 2.5%, up slightly from the previous 2.4%.
These numbers will provide more hints about what to expect from the Federal Reserve. Economists expect the bank to leave rates unchanged in this meeting and then hike in December.
The stock market will also react to the bond market , where the ten-year and five-year yields have pulled back in the past few days.
The US stock market will also react to the ongoing crisis in the Middle East, which is escalating. For example, Yemen’s Houthis launched a major attack against Saudi Arabia’s main airport, with President Donald Trump hinting that the US will intervene .
Iran has also continued striking ships crossing the Strait of Hormuz. There is also fear that the crisis will escalate. If this happens, it means that crude oil prices will continue rising in the coming weeks. This, in turn, will lead to higher inflation, pushing the Fed to hike interest rates, which may affect the stock market.
Nvidia-Backed Neoclouds Face a Test as IREN, CoreWeave and Nebius Stocks Slide
Top Nvidia-backed neocloud companies like IREN, CoreWeave and Nebius are facing a major rest as they remain in a bear market.

Business News &rsaquo; News &rsaquo; International &rsaquo; US News &rsaquo; US Stock market on Columbus day predictions: S&P 500, Dow Jones, Nasdaq, Russell 2000 investors eye tricky Monday on Indigenous Peoples' Day. Nvidia, Apple, Micron, AMD, Tesla other stocks to watch out today

US Stock market on Columbus day predictions: S&P 500, Dow Jones, Nasdaq, Russell 2000 investors eye tricky Monday on Indigenous Peoples' Day. Nvidia, Apple, Micron, AMD, Tesla other stocks to watch out today
US Stock market on Columbus day predictions: S&P 500, Dow Jones, Nasdaq, Russell 2000 investors eye tricky Monday on Indigenous Peoples' Day. Nvidia, Apple, Micron, AMD, Tesla other stocks to watch out today
, Global Desk Last Updated: Oct 12, 2026, 04:17:00 PM IST
Stock market on Columbus day: Earnings season begins on Tuesday, with major US banks including Wells Fargo, Goldman Sachs, Citigroup and JPMorgan Chase scheduled to report first.
AP All three major indexes -- S&P 500, Dow Jones, Russell 2000 -- ended higher last week on optimism around an AI-driven third-quarter earnings season. (AP Photo)
US Stock market is set to open on a mixed note on Columbus day as Wall St futures were by and large muted on Monday. S&P 500 futures were up slightly at 7,864.50, Dow Jones futures rose marginally at 51,948. However, Nasdaq futures were down in the pre-market trading at 31,072. Russell 2000 futures fell too at 2,821. While stock markets were open on Columbus Day or Indigenous Peoples' Day , the US bond market will not be operational on October 12.
Wall Street investors monitored oil prices and geopolitical developments ahead of key bank earnings and inflation data due this week that could influence the US Federal Reserve's interest-rate path.
Oil prices swung between gains and losses after President Donald Trump said Ukraine and Russia had agreed to halt attacks on each other's energy facilities following a US diesel-supply deal with Moscow. Details of the agreement were not clear, however, and the plan faces opposition in Washington. In other dealings early Monday, the U.S. dollar rose to 158.43 Japanese yen from 158.23 yen. The euro was unchanged at $1.1191.
The Middle East conflict also kept Brent crude above $100 a barrel after deadly strikes on Riyadh's international airport over the weekend.
Iran-US war: Is key Donald Trump ally Saudi Arabia losing battle against Iran-backed Houthis in Yemen?
"Any hope that Trump's deal with Putin might help bring down prices has been negated by the flare-up in the Houthi conflict," said Chris Beauchamp, chief market analyst at IG.
"While earnings season will be a key driver this week, investors should expect oil news to keep making headlines, weighing heavily on sentiment."
Earnings season begins on Tuesday, with major US banks including Wells Fargo, Goldman Sachs, Citigroup and JPMorgan Chase scheduled to report first.
Gold price prediction for Monday, October 12: Check latest gold rate predictions
S&P 500 earnings are expected to jump 30.6 per cent in the third quarter, according to LSEG IBES data. All three major indexes -- S&P 500, Dow Jones, Russell 2000 -- ended higher last week on optimism around an AI-driven third-quarter earnings season and hope that the Fed may avoid near-term rate hikes offsetting sharp swings in oil and Treasury markets.
AI chipmaker Nvidia, the world's most valuable company, has contributed almost 2 per cent to the benchmark's gain this year, followed by contributions of over 1 per cent each from Apple , Micron and Advanced Micro Devices. Tesla's 15 per cent dip this year has subtracted about 0.3 per cent from the S&P 500's rally.
The US Treasury market is closed for Columbus Day. Treasury yields climbed to 24-year highs earlier this month as elevated energy prices stoked concerns inflation could remain sticky, potentially keeping policymakers hawkish and pressuring equity valuations.
Investors will also be watching this week's US consumer and producer price data as well as retail sales figures. Higher energy prices are expected to push headline CPI up 0.6 per cent on the month and 3.7 per cent annually, while core inflation is seen rising at a more modest 0.2 per cent monthly and 2.5 per cent annually.
Markets are pricing in just a 19 per cent chance the central bank will raise interest rates this month, according to CME's FedWatch tool. In premarket movers, IREN's shares jumped 5.4 per cent after The Information reported Anthropic had entered into a multi-year cloud deal with the data-center firm.

- **发生了什么**: Iran maintains indirect comms as it weighs US' Hormuz plan reply&nbsp;&nbsp;Daily Sabah

<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Iran maintains indirect comms as it weighs US' Hormuz plan reply | Daily Sabah
Iran maintains indirect comms as it weighs US' Hormuz plan reply
People walks past a mural depicting Iran&#039;s late Supreme Leader Ayatollah Ali Khamenei, in Tehran, Iran, Oct. 7, 2026. (Reuters Photo)
Iran continues to review a U.S. response to its proposal to reopen the Strait of Hormuz and will reply through intermediaries once the review is complete, Foreign Ministry spokesperson Esmaeil Baghaei said Monday.
Baghaei also ​told a press conference that ​Iran ⁠continues to exchange messages via intermediaries, without elaborating. Qatar and Pakistan, among others, have acted as mediators in efforts to end the war that began with U.S. and Israeli strikes on Iran on Feb. 28.
"The exchange of messages between Iran and the U.S. through mediators continues," Baghaei said.
"We are reviewing the Americans’ proposals for negotiations. Iran’s preconditions must be observed for any negotiations," he added.
After a June interim cease-fire crumbled and on-off strikes resumed, Iran presented its proposal in mid-September and later confirmed receipt of a U.S. response.
However, ⁠the process has since stalled, with Iran’s security chief saying Sunday that Washington’s response did not sufficiently address Tehran’s seven conditions for reopening the Strait of Hormuz and returning to negotiations.
A central point of disagreement has been Washington’s insistence on addressing Iran&#039;s nuclear program from the outset.
Tehran ⁠says negotiations should initially focus on reopening the Strait of Hormuz, which before the war carried about a fifth of ​the global trade in oil and liquefied natural gas (LNG) and ​restoring security in the waterway.
Baqhaei added that Russia also continues to play a "constructive" role regarding Iran’s nuclear file, pointing to Moscow’s involvement in the 2015 nuclear agreement.
He said Iranian and Russian relations are based on mutual trust and that Tehran welcomes supportive efforts by friendly countries, but stressed that, "technically and precisely," mediation between Iran and the U.S. remains in the hands of countries such as Pakistan and Qatar.
Turning to the Strait of Hormuz, Baqhaei said developments there were a direct consequence of U.S. and Israeli attacks on Iran and rejected suggestions that Tehran’s actions were intended to create negotiating leverage.
"What we have seen in the Strait of Hormuz since Feb. 28 was the direct result of the U.S. and the Israeli regime&#039;s attack on Iran," he said.
"With the current U.S. approach, security will not return," he added.
Baqhaei rejected U.S. assertions that Iran had lost its leverage, saying such claims did not correspond with realities on the ground.
"They can say Iran has lost its leverage, but we are doing our work," he said, arguing that energy markets were not responding to Washington’s assertions.
Baqhaei also said that Iran was "deeply concerned" by escalating attacks between Yemen&#039;s Tehran-backed Houthis and Saudi Arabia, after 14 people died in two days of strikes on Riyadh&#039;s airport.
"We are deeply concerned about the developments taking place in the Arabian Peninsula," he said, adding that Tehran was ready "to help facilitate dialogue between the parties."
A Houthi strike on Riyadh&#039;s international airport on Sunday killed one person and wounded 11 others, a day after 13 died in the deadliest strike on Saudi soil in more than a decade.
"We absolutely do not want hostilities and war to continue between two Islamic countries," Baqhaei said, urging the parties to resolve the situation "through dialogue and engagement."
The Houthis are part of the Iran-backed "Axis of Resistance," a network of groups opposed to Israel that also includes Lebanon&#039;s Hezbollah and Iraqi Shiite armed groups.

### 41. ★★★☆☆ Microsoft reveals Maia 300 AI chip, as it works to reduce dependence on Nvidia, AMD - Yahoo Finance

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: GN AI
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-12T13:00:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Microsoft reveals Maia 300 AI chip, as it works to reduce dependence on Nvidia, AMD&nbsp;&nbsp;Yahoo Finance</

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Microsoft reveals Maia 300 AI chip as it works to reduce dependence on Nvidia, AMD
Microsoft ( MSFT ) has debuted its latest AI chip, the Azure Maia 300, as it looks to reduce its reliance on third-party processors from the likes of Nvidia ( NVDA ) and AMD ( AMD ).
Unveiled at the OCP Global Summit 2026 in San Jose on Monday, the Maia 300 will join Microsoft's Maia 200 and Maia 100 chips as part of what the company said will be multigigawatt deployments beginning next year.
The Windows maker said that will make its Maia chips the "primary AI inference system for Microsoft products and services."
The company said Maia 300 provides 40% more tokens per megawatt than "leading GPU-based systems," a clear swipe at Nvidia, and that a 96-chip rack offers seven times the rack-scale token throughput of Microsoft's Maia 200.
Tokens are a basic unit of measurement in AI systems that can represent parts of words, images, and videos.
The more tokens per megawatt, the more efficient an AI rack.
Microsoft's Maia 300 processor. (Image: Microsoft) · Microsoft The Maia 200 chip currently powers its Microsoft production workloads, including Microsoft 365 Copilot, as well as OpenAI ( OPAI.PVT ) models, Microsoft's MAI models, and open-source models. That provides 1.7 times more tokens per watt for AI capabilities in Microsoft's Teams, Copilot, and Cowork software versus other GPU-based systems, the company said.
Microsoft and fellow hyperscaler peers Amazon ( AMZN ), Google ( GOOG , GOOGL ), and Meta ( META ), as well as AI labs OpenAI and Anthropic ( ANTH.PVT ), either already offer or are working on their own custom chips.
Doing so acts as a hedge against becoming overdependent on processors from Nvidia and AMD. Producing their own semiconductors also allows companies to tailor them to their specific needs.
That's a potential problem for Nvidia, which regularly generates upwards of 50% of its quarterly revenue from hyperscalers.
On top of that, Amazon and Google now rent out their custom chips to third-party customers, a direct threat to Nvidia's business. What's more, Amazon is looking into selling chips for customers to install in their own data centers rather than running them from Amazon's own facilities.
That doesn't necessarily mean hyperscalers will stop purchasing Nvidia chips entirely. Customers still want access to a number of chips for different workloads, meaning the Amazons and Microsofts of the world will need to continue stocking up on the most in-demand processors.

### 42. ★★★☆☆ Microsoft makes ‘biggest bet yet’ on its own silicon, unveils new AI chip to reduce reliance on Nvidia - GeekWire

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: GN AI
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-12T13:22:17+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Microsoft makes ‘biggest bet yet’ on its own silicon, unveils new AI chip to reduce reliance on Nvidia&nbsp;&nbsp;GeekWire

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

by Todd Bishop on Oct 12, 2026 at 6:22 am October 12, 2026 at 6:24 am
A prototype of Microsoft’s Azure Maia 300 chip, which the company plans to begin deploying at scale in 2027. (Microsoft Photo)
Microsoft plans to run most of its AI services on chips it designs itself, a shift that could save the company billions of dollars and reduce its dependence on industry juggernaut Nvidia.
The company on Monday unveiled Maia 300, the third generation of its custom AI chip, saying it will begin large-scale deployments in 2027. In a blog post , Azure chief Scott Guthrie said Maia will become “the primary AI inference system for Microsoft products and services.”
Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella called it “our biggest bet yet on our own silicon” in a post on X .
Maia 300 will produce 40% more AI output for the same amount of power as leading GPU-based systems, which Nvidia dominates, according to the company.
A full rack of the new chips will produce up to seven times as much AI output as the current generation, the company says. They’re designed for the kinds of tasks that are growing fastest, such as AI agents that work through multistep jobs and models that handle long documents or conversations.
The Redmond company designs the chips and relies on Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co. (TSMC) to make them, rather than buying finished chips from Nvidia. More-efficient chips also let Microsoft run AI services using less electricity, at a time when power supply has become one of the biggest constraints on AI expansion.
Microsoft and Nvidia remain big partners. Nadella and Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang shared the stage last week to launch Windows PCs built on Nvidia chips. Nvidia also remains a key supplier for Microsoft’s data centers, and Nadella said on the company’s July earnings call that Microsoft will be among the first cloud providers to deploy next-generation systems based on Nvidia’s Vera Rubin and AMD’s Helios.
Microsoft’s move is part of a larger trend among tech giants to design their own AI chips. Google has started selling its Tensor Processing Units directly to some customers, and Amazon has won over AI companies including Anthropic with its Trainium chips. Meta has rolled out its own AI chips , and OpenAI unveiled its first custom chip , built with Broadcom, in June.
Microsoft has been playing catch-up. Unlike Google’s and Amazon’s chips, Maia isn’t available for outside customers to rent. Microsoft’s first Maia chip, announced in 2023 , was followed by a second-generation Maia 200 this January after delays. As of this summer, Maia 200 was running in just two U.S. data centers, The Information reported in August .
Maia 200, the current generation, already powers Microsoft 365 Copilot and runs models from OpenAI as well as Microsoft’s own. It’s now being deployed across the U.S., Europe and Australia, according to Microsoft. GitHub Copilot and Microsoft Foundry are set to move onto it by the end of the year.
The shift will take time. Evercore ISI analysts estimate that Maia will account for about 10% of Microsoft’s spending on AI chips by early 2027, CNBC reported .
Microsoft says it will share more details next month at its Ignite conference in San Francisco.

### 43. ★★★☆☆ Microsoft’s new AI chip makes it less reliant on Nvidia’s GPUs - The Verge

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: GN AI
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-12T13:00:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Microsoft’s new AI chip makes it less reliant on Nvidia’s GPUs&nbsp;&nbsp;The Verge

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Microsoft’s new AI chip makes it less reliant on Nvidia’s GPUs
The new Maia 300 chip will start rolling out to production AI workloads in 2027.
The new Maia 300 chip will start rolling out to production AI workloads in 2027.
Tom Warren is a senior correspondent and author of Notepad , who has been covering all things Microsoft, PC, and tech for over 20 years.
Microsoft is announcing its third-generation Maia in-house AI chip today, designed to be the “primary AI inference system” for Microsoft’s products and services. Maia 300 not only improves the cost and efficiency of running AI workloads in the cloud, it’s also more capable of handling the latest reasoning models and agentic tasks.
Maia 300 will start being rolled out to production in 2027, and it has been designed with efficiency in mind. “We expect Maia 300 to deliver 40 percent more tokens per megawatt compared to leading GPU-based systems,” says Scott Guthrie , executive vice president of Microsoft’s cloud group. “And in a full 96-accelerator configuration, it delivers up to 7x the rack-scale token throughput of Maia 200.”
These efficiency improvements will allow Microsoft to rely mainly on its in-house AI chips soon, instead of ones from Nvidia and AMD. “We will scale our Azure Maia family to multi-gigawatt deployments starting in 2027, making Maia the primary AI inference system for Microsoft products and services,” says Guthrie.
Maia 300 chips will start being used for production workloads in 2027. Image: Microsoft
Maia 300 is a successor to the 200, which was only announced in January . Maia 200 is already running on production AI workloads across Microsoft, including Microsoft Copilot, GitHub Copilot, Microsoft Foundry, and Microsoft’s own AI models.
“Maia 200 is the best performing AI infrastructure in our fleet, measured in real world metrics for AI services,” says Guthrie. “Maia 300 is a complete AI system designed for the new types of AI workloads emerging today: Long-context reasoning, latency-sensitive conversational experiences, and sustained, multi-step agentic tasks.”
Microsoft is finally making custom chips — and they’re all about AI
Microsoft’s latest AI chip goes head-to-head with Amazon and Google
Microsoft is using a new chiplet architecture to get its all-important efficiency gains. Built on TSMC’s N2P process node, the latest Maia 300 architecture separates compute, memory controller, and I/O functions. “This increases the silicon area dedicated to compute performance and allows each function to use the technology best suited to its role,” says the Azure hardware and infrastructure team.
Maia 300 is also a key part of Microsoft’s own AI model work, and could help the company achieve its goal of catching up with Google DeepMind, OpenAI, and Anthropic. “We have to work across the full stack, all the way down to the silicon,” says Microsoft AI CEO Mustafa Suleyman. “At that scale we need a platform that we own, and that we co-design with our models. That’s why running our Microsoft AI models on top of the Maia chip is a critical focus for our team.”

### 44. ★★★☆☆ Microsoft challenges Nvidia with new Maia 300 AI chip promising 40% greater efficiency - Ynetnews

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: GN AI
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-12T14:08:08+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Microsoft challenges Nvidia with new Maia 300 AI chip promising 40% greater efficiency&nbsp;&nbsp;Ynetnews

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Microsoft challenges Nvidia with new Maia 300 AI chip promising 40% greater efficienc
Microsoft challenges Nvidia with new Maia 300 AI chip promising 40% greater efficiency
Microsoft says its new in-house AI accelerator can generate 40% more tokens using the same power as competing GPU-based systems; deployment is set to begin in 2027, with the chip expected to become a central platform for the company's AI services
Microsoft on Monday unveiled Maia 300, a powerful new artificial intelligence chip developed in-house, along with a complete computing system designed to support it, as the technology giant steps up efforts to reduce its dependence on Nvidia and secure greater control over the infrastructure powering its AI services.
The company says the new chip and the system built around it can deliver 40% more AI output at the same level of electricity consumption as competing systems based on graphics processing units, or GPUs. Microsoft plans to begin deploying Maia 300 in 2027, when it is expected to become a central platform for running AI models across its products.
The announcement marks another step in the intensifying competition among technology giants to develop their own AI processors rather than rely heavily on Nvidia, whose chips continue to dominate the market for training advanced AI models.
Maia 300 is a specialized data center processor known as an AI accelerator. It was designed by Microsoft to run AI workloads in the massive data centers supporting its Azure cloud platform, with an emphasis on processing information quickly while using as little electricity as possible.
Microsoft is following a path already taken by Google, which developed its own Tensor Processing Units, or TPUs, and Amazon, which created its Trainium chips. All three companies are seeking to gain greater control over the hardware powering their growing AI operations.
For Microsoft, developing its own processors is also a way to prepare for surging global demand for AI computing power, reduce supply chain bottlenecks and secure a reliable supply of chips without depending entirely on external suppliers.
One of the main reasons behind Maia 300 is the rapid rise of AI agents, systems capable of carrying out complex tasks involving multiple steps rather than simply answering individual questions.
These agents require computing infrastructure capable of handling enormous amounts of information, responding quickly and operating efficiently over extended periods. Microsoft says Maia 300 was designed specifically for these increasingly demanding workloads.
From right: Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang and Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella. The companies remain close partners even as Microsoft works to reduce its dependence on Nvidia's chips
Unlike chips primarily used to train AI models, Maia 300 focuses on a process known as inference. This is the stage at which an already trained model processes a user&#x27;s request and generates a response.
Training involves teaching a model to recognize patterns and develop capabilities using vast amounts of data. Inference is what happens afterward, when that model is put to work answering questions, analyzing information or completing tasks.
Nvidia&#x27;s processors still dominate the training market, but Microsoft is positioning Maia 300 as a more efficient alternative for inference, particularly as AI agents become more capable and their computing requirements grow.
According to Microsoft, the new accelerator and its supporting system can produce 40% more tokens at the same power consumption as competing GPU-based platforms. Tokens are the basic units of text that AI models process and generate, making token throughput a key measure of performance for AI services.
Another major focus of Maia 300 is reducing the time needed to move data between the different components of an AI computing system.
In large AI models, information must constantly travel between memory and processors, as well as between multiple accelerators working together. These transfers can create bottlenecks that limit performance even when the processors themselves are extremely powerful.
Microsoft says Maia 300 addresses this problem with an architecture designed to speed up data movement and improve efficiency across the entire system.
The chip provides memory bandwidth of 18 terabytes per second, allowing it to feed large volumes of information to AI models at high speed and support workloads that require substantial memory capacity.
The company says these capabilities will be particularly important for the next generation of AI agents, which will increasingly need to analyze extensive information, maintain context across long interactions and complete complex, multistep assignments.
Maia 300 is not Microsoft&#x27;s first attempt to develop its own AI hardware. The company already operates an earlier system, Maia 200, which supports its Copilot AI assistant and the team developing its next generation of AI models.
Microsoft plans to expand the earlier system&#x27;s use to additional services by the end of 2026, before beginning deployment of Maia 300 in 2027.
The transition reflects a broader shift in the AI industry. As artificial intelligence becomes embedded in everyday software and AI agents take on more demanding tasks, the cost of running models is becoming increasingly important alongside the computing power required to train them.
For Microsoft, the potential benefits extend beyond performance. Developing its own chips could reduce reliance on Nvidia, ease pressure on hardware supply chains and give the company more control over the costs and availability of computing resources.
The two companies remain major partners, with Nvidia&#x27;s technology playing an important role in Microsoft&#x27;s AI infrastructure. But Maia 300 highlights Microsoft&#x27;s determination to build an increasingly independent hardware ecosystem.
Whether the new chip can meaningfully alter the balance of power in the AI semiconductor market will depend on how it performs once deployment begins in 2027.

Zacks Investment Ideas feature highlights: Qualcomm, Alphabet, Tesla, AMD, Amazon, Taiwan Semiconductor, NVIDIA, Intel, Apple, Broadcom and SpaceX
Chicago, IL – October 12, 2026 – Today, Zacks Investment Ideas feature highlights Qualcomm QCOM, Alphabet GOOGL, Tesla TSLA, Advanced Micro Devices AMD, Amazon AMZN, Taiwan Semiconductor (TSMC), NVIDIA NVDA, Intel INTC, Apple AAPL, Broadcom AVGO and SpaceX SPCX.
Record Profits at Samsung & TSMC Say the AI Trade Is Intact
Despite a questionable macro economy and soaring energy prices, equity markets have been buoyed by the massive, unprecedented artificial intelligence buildout. Just how impactful has the AI revolution been for the U.S. economy and stock market?
The massive wave of CAPEX spending on data centers, graphics processing units (GPUs), and energy has accounted for more than a quarter of total U.S. GDP growth, and now dwarfs every other major American infrastructure boom, including the Interstate Highway System, the Apollo space program, and the 19th-century railroad expansion. In other words, Wall Street's big question is: "How sustainable is AI spending?" The answer matters not only to the AI industry, but also for the U.S. economy and stock market as a whole.
Below are three clues that AI spending is sustainable for the foreseeable future, including:
Samsung Electronics is the world's largest memory manufacturer. AI data centers have driven insatiable demand for memory over the past few years. Last night, Samsung's earnings gave investors clues about AI spending. Samsung reported the most profitable quarter ever by any company ($80.2 billion). Samsung's main customers include Qualcomm , Alphabet, Tesla , Advanced Micro Devices and Amazon .
Memory makers aren't the only ones showing that AI growth remains scorching-hot. Taiwan Semiconductor , the world's largest manufacturer of AI chips, reported record Q3 revenue of $46.7 billion, up 50% year over year. TSM is a critical AI company to watch because it produces over 60% of all global contract chips and over 90% of advanced chips. NVIDIA , AMD, QCOM, Intel , Apple and Broadcom are all TSM customers.
Earlier this week, SpaceX announced it will raise $40 billion in debt to buy NVIDIA chips as it scales its AI compute. Although investors often frown upon debt offerings, SpaceX's compute economics are mind-boggling. For every dollar SpaceX spends bringing on AI compute, it's payback period is well under one year, thanks to lucrative deals with Google and Anthropic. In other words, SpaceX is proving that n AI compute investing not only makes sense, but the economics are also extremely attractive.
The sustainability question about the AI infrastructure buildout has been answered. Recent news from Samsung, TSMC, and SpaceX suggests record CAPEX spending will continue.

According to the Oct. 12 contract on Polymarket , a Polygon (CRYPTO: POL) based prediction platform, traders are heavily bullish on the S&P 500 open. The “S&P 500 (SPX) Up or Down on October 12?” contract currently reflects a 78% chance of a higher open.
Traders are leaning into positive index futures while evaluating Middle East headlines and incoming Q3 corporate earnings:
Positive Index Futures: The stock index futures indicate a green open across major benchmarks. Dow Jones futures are leading the advance, up 0.84%. S&P 500 futures rose 0.56%. Nasdaq 100 futures gained 0.45%, and Russell 2000 futures added 0.33%.
Cooling Oil Relieves Rate Pressures: Energy prices pulled back in early trading. Brent crude fell 1.27% to $103.39 a barrel, while WTI crude dropped 1.61% to $90.37 a barrel. The pullbacks offer relief following news that Iran rejected Washington’s latest response to its peace proposal as “insufficient” and threatened critical digital infrastructure across the region if fighting intensifies.
Big Bank Earnings Season Arrives: Wall Street’s focus is shifting to third-quarter corporate results. Major financial institutions—including JPMorgan Chase & Co. (NYSE: JPM ), Bank of America Corp. Investors will be watching executive commentary on consumer health and net interest income amid elevated interest rates.
Economic Calendar: The bond market is closed on Monday for Columbus Day, though the equity market remains open. Later this week, investors will digest September Consumer Price Index (CPI) inflation data, retail sales figures, and the Federal Reserve’s Beige Book.
Equities maintain strong momentum after all major stock indexes closed higher on Friday. As detailed in market commentary from Ed Yardeni , last week saw a notable shift as interest-rate-sensitive sectors led gains while Treasury yields edged down. Consensus expectations for third-quarter S&P 500 earnings growth have been revised upward to 30.6% year-over-year, with all 11 S&P 500 sectors projected to report positive revenue and profit growth simultaneously.

### 52. ★★★☆☆ Pentagon Finally Counts an Iran War Death — Six Months Late - The Intercept

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-12T13:02:11+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Pentagon Finally Counts an Iran War Death — Six Months Late&nbsp;&nbsp;The Intercept

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

An Army carry team moves the flag-draped transfer case containing the remains of U.S. Army soldier Maj. Sorffly Davius, who died in Kuwait, during a casualty return on March 9, 2026, at Dover Air Force Base, Del.
The Pentagon quietly acknowledged additional Iran war deaths on Friday evening after The Intercept repeatedly demanded an explanation for inaccurate statistics on a War Department website tracking U.S. casualties from the conflict.
Almost 900 U.S. troops have been wounded or killed in the war with Iran, according to the official Pentagon count, including the two deaths added on Friday. This raises the Pentagon’s total to 21 dead.
The additional war dead include Maj. Sorffly Davius, a signals and communication officer with the New York Army National Guard who was assigned to the headquarters of the 42nd Infantry Division and died while on duty in Camp Buehring, Kuwait, on March 6. For more than six months, The Intercept has raised Davius’s case in dozens of emails and phone conversations with Pentagon officials and publicly in 10 articles.
The Pentagon recently revamped the website of the Defense Casualty Analysis System, or DCAS — which provides official counts of dead and wounded for Congress and the White House — to include more detailed casualty category tables. But it initially listed no “non-hostile” illness or injury fatalities, despite the Pentagon announcing in March that Davius died “as a result of a non-combat related incident.”
At first, the military claimed that Davius was involved in another operation instead of Epic Fury — the original designation of the Iran war. Two weeks ago, however, The Intercept revealed that a recent National Guard news release admitted that when “President Donald Trump made the decision to attack Iran and launched Operation Epic Fury on Feb. 28, the [42nd Infantry] division headquarters began conducting combat operations.” The 42nd served as the primary headquarters leading what became, according to Maj. Gen. Jack James, the division commander, the “largest and first long-range precision fires campaign in the history of the United States Army.” James specifically saluted Davius, whom the Guard admitted was “conducting operations” when he died.
Last week, The Intercept pointed the Pentagon to the non-hostile illness or injury casualty count for Epic Fury on the website, and emphasized its tally showing zero deaths was not possible.
“I will check within,” Patricia Kreuzberger, a Pentagon spokesperson said on Tuesday. “Let me reach out to leadership.”
Three days later, and after repeated follow-up messages on Friday, the tally was updated, to 1.

ICE Shooting in NYC Inflames Mamdani’s Policing Problem
He Confessed to an Afghanistan War Murder. The U.S. Military Promptly Closed the Case.
In addition to Davius, the DCAS death count now also includes an unidentified Navy suicide that occurred last month, according to a U.S. official who spoke on the condition of anonymity. DCAS currently lists this death as “pending,” despite the recent addition of a “self-inflicted” category to the website.
A “War Department official” told The Intercept by email on Sunday that “the Department is introducing a ‘Pending Adjudication’ category to track the status of active duty casualties pending final categorization and reporting in DCAS.” She added: “This new category will allow the Department to minimize reporting delays while ensuring data consistency between the DoW and Joint Staff.”
The “pending” designation, however, isn’t new. The Pentagon re-introduced it to DCAS in mid-July, and major discrepancies have continued in the tally since.
Many casualties remain uncounted in DCAS, including a sailor who suffered a  non-combat-related injury  aboard the USS Abraham Lincoln as it was involved in “strike missions in support of Operation Epic Fury” on March 25.
The Pentagon Found a New Way to Undercount U.S. Casualties in Iran
Most Iran war casualties in the official count — 454 personnel as of Friday — have occurred since July 7, after the Trump administration claimed the conflict ended. At that time, the Pentagon switched from counting casualties under Epic Fury to the anodyne moniker “Overseas Operations.”
The most recent casualties are three Army personnel wounded in action this month. The latest known Iran war fatality is Capt. Bianca Wilkerson, 37, of Norfolk, Virginia, who died on September 18 from a “coronary issue,” according to her brother. He said she returned from Saudi Arabia on September 10 and was headed back but died on the return flight, according to reporting by the Virginian-Pilot. A U.S. official told The Intercept that Wilkerson died “as a result of a non-combat-related incident,” but after nearly a month, DCAS still lists no non-hostile illness or injury deaths.
“There is no excuse for a mistake of this magnitude,” Reps. Chrissy Houlahan, D-Pa., and Adam Smith, D-Wash., said in a joint statement on Friday after the House Armed Services Committee discovered “a discrepancy in the Pentagon’s reporting of casualty numbers” and brought it to “the Trump Administration’s attention.” They continued: “The failure of Secretary Hegseth to immediately take ownership of the problem also does a grave disservice to the men and women putting their lives on the line in service to our country.”

For six months , The Intercept has reported on anomalies in official counts offered on DCAS. When the first ceasefire was struck between the Trump administration and Iran on April 7, the tally of U.S. casualties was 385. Despite a pause in hostilities, the number slowly rose to 428, according to Pentagon statistics. But on April 21, the number of  wounded-in-action troops declined by 15  without public acknowledgment by the War Department. Despite repeated questions for months, the Pentagon has not explained the adjustment.
Other discrepancies have also gone unjustified for months. An Army “illness/injury” death appeared on or about May 26 but disappeared by June 1. On July 20, the Pentagon repeatedly altered DCAS, adding 100 casualties to the war’s tally without explanation; the total number of wounded cited by DCAS on that date included counts of 413, 427, and 447. Three days later, the Pentagon dropped the Iran war death toll from 18 to 14. On July 22, DCAS added 10 recent casualties to the Epic Fury count. By July 25, those wounded personnel were dropped from the tally. They may have been added to the Overseas Operations casualty toll, but the Pentagon has failed to offer any explanations.
U.S. Has Suffered More Casualties in Iran Since the Conclusion of Operation Epic Fury
Prior reporting by The Intercept also found that the Pentagon’s official tally of dead and wounded personnel is a gross undercount, stemming from what one U.S. government official called, in April, a “ casualty cover-up .” When the Washington Post also suggested last month that the Pentagon had suppressed the casualty numbers, self-styled War Secretary Pete Hegseth called the report “DISGUSTING and FAKE.”
When asked by The Intercept about the Pentagon’s continued peddling of bogus statistics, Kreuzberger did not offer an explanation. An email from a nameless “War Department official” said: "We have nothing to provide beyond the Defense Casualty Analysis System website."
What we’re seeing right now from Donald Trump is a full-on authoritarian takeover of the U.S. government.
Court orders are being ignored. MAGA loyalists have been put in charge of the military and federal law enforcement agencies. The Department of Government Efficiency has stripped Congress of its power of the purse. News outlets that challenge Trump have been banished or put under investigation.
Yet far too many are still covering Trump’s assault on democracy like politics as usual, with flattering headlines describing Trump as “unconventional,” “testing the boundaries,” and “aggressively flexing power.”
The Intercept has long covered authoritarian governments, billionaire oligarchs, and backsliding democracies around the world. We understand the challenge we face in Trump and the vital importance of press freedom in defending democracy.

Corporate news outlets have cowered, becoming accessories in Trump’s project to create a post-truth America. Right-wing billionaires have pounced, buying up media organizations and rebuilding the information environment to their liking.
In this most perilous moment for democracy, The Intercept is fighting back. But to do so effectively, we need to grow.

Follow updates on the war in the Middle East for Monday, October 12, here . See earlier developments below.
There was another strike on Riyadh's King Khalid International Airport on Sunday, after an attack the previous day killed at least 12 people and wounded another 309, according to Saudi authorities. An American was among those victims. The Iran-backed Houthis have claimed responsibility for the strikes.
President Trump on Saturday left open the possibility the U.S. will join Saudi Arabia's battle against the Houthis. "We may. We're gonna look at it," Mr. Trump said. Fighting between the Iranian proxy group and Saudi-backed forces in Yemen appears to be the new epicenter of the wider war .
President Trump spoke with Russian leader Vladimir Putin on Friday to discuss freeing up millions of tons of diesel , but the Russian president also relayed Iran's position on a peace deal to his American counterpart, according to the Kremlin.
Saudi authorities say King Fahd International Airport in Dammam was attacked
Saudi's General Authority of Civil Aviation on Sunday confirmed that King Fahd International Airport had been attacked after the Iran-backed Houthis said they had targeted the airport in Dammam.
It's unclear whether there were any injuries. The Saudi civil aviation authority said it is assessing the damage .
King Fahd International Airport is located approximately 400 miles away from the King Khalid International Airport in Riyadh, which was also attacked on Sunday.
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-trump-us-elections-saudi-arabia-yemen-houthis/#post-update-070a5f14
Saudi, Egypt, Pakistan and Turkey condemn Houthi strikes on Saudi Arabia
Saudi Arabia, Egypt, Pakistan and Turkey on Sunday issued a joint statement condemning the Houthis' attacks against Saudi Arabia, including the latest strike on Riyadh's King Khalid International Airport.
The regional group of four nations said the attacks in Riyadh, which killed at least 12 people and wounded another 309 the previous day, "constitute a blatant violation of international law."
An American was among those victims. Saudi Arabia's General Authority of Civil Aviation said those killed also included four Saudi citizens, two Bangladeshis and one person each from Egypt, Jordan, Syria, Palestine and Sudan.
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-trump-us-elections-saudi-arabia-yemen-houthis/#post-update-705b8589
Iran calls U.S. response "insufficient" amid stalled talks to end the war
The head of Iran's Supreme National Security Council said Tehran considers the latest U.S. response "insufficient," amid stalled negotiations to end the war and fully open the Strait of Hormuz.
The comments by Mohsen Rezaei, a former Revolutionary Guard commander, were reported by Iran's state-run IRNA news agency.
Rezaei added that Iran is no longer responding to the U.S. and said Washington must clarify certain issues, without giving details. He said the U.S. has accepted some of the seven conditions but others remain unclear.
Iran's delegation to the annual U.N. gathering of world leaders last month proposed a sped-up agreement that echoed the memorandum of understanding that collapsed days after the U.S. and Iran signed it in June. The new proposal was rejected, but the two sides have remained in contact via mediation.
Rezaei also threatened "cables, data centers, AI facilities and other sites in the region" if intense fighting breaks out again.
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-trump-us-elections-saudi-arabia-yemen-houthis/#post-update-ac11baa4
Houthis claim Saudi airport attacks, including new strike on Sunday
Another projectile struck a passenger terminal at the international airport in Riyadh on Sunday, according to the Saudi-led coalition fighting Iran-backed Houthi rebels in Yemen. The latest attack came one day after a missile hit the same airport terminal, killing 12 people and injuring hundreds.
Details about the latest strike on the King Khalid International Airport complex weren't immediately available, outside of the fact that terminals 3 and 4, for domestic flights, were affected. The missile attack Saturday was the deadliest in a Gulf Arab nation since the Iran war began. Of around 300 people wounded in its aftermath, some suffered critical injuries. The airport was closed Sunday.
The newest attack happened as the Houthis said they carried out at least four operations targeting the airport in Riyadh since Saturday. Houthi military spokesman Brig. Gen. Yahya Saree said on Telegram that the attacks came in response to "brutal Saudi aggression against our country and our people," CBS News partner BBC News reported . He claimed Saudi Arabia launched more than 120 airstrikes in the past 24 hours in parts of Yemen under Houthi control.
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-trump-us-elections-saudi-arabia-yemen-houthis/#post-update-39e81e9f
Sen. Chris Murphy warns against U.S. retaliating on Saudi's behalf amid war "spiraling out of control"
Democratic Sen. Chris Murphy, of Connecticut, said the U.S. shouldn't retaliate on Saudi Arabia's behalf following the strike at Riyadh's King Khalid International Airport, arguing that going to war with the Houthis in Yemen would be an escalation with dangerous consequences.
"This administration is absolutely incompetent in war-making, and so just as we have made the Middle East worse and more chaotic by getting involved in a war against Iran, we would simply escalate and make the situation even more dangerous," Murphy said on "Face the Nation with Margaret Brennan."
Murphy, who said he was at the Riyadh airport the day before the strike, said, "What I saw on the ground is a war that is spiraling out of control."
Murphy cited the 12 people killed in the airport strike, a "full-blown new war" between the Houthis in Yemen and Saudi Arabia, the Iranians restarting their missile programs, and evidence that he said suggests they're restarting nuclear research activities, along with the deaths of American service members and the war's high price tag.
"If the Trump administration was trying to lose this war, they couldn't do worse than they are doing," Murphy said. "This is a humiliation to the United States. The war is getting bigger and more dangerous, not smaller and more limited."
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-trump-us-elections-saudi-arabia-yemen-houthis/#post-update-5cb4be7d
Netanyahu denounces Riyadh attack, calls for a region "united against threats"
Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu spoke out Sunday in the wake of the deadly attack on Saudi Arabia's Riyadh airport, joining leaders from other countries in the region and beyond who've condemned it. The U.S., Israel and other nations have blamed Yemen's Houthis for the strike, although the group hasn't claimed responsibility for it outright.
"Terrorism targets innocent people and seeks to spread fear and instability. It must be confronted with resolve," said Netanyahu in a statement posted to X . "Israel looks forward to a strong Middle East&mdash;one that stands united against threats, protects its people, and builds a future of security, cooperation, and prosperity for all the peoples of our region."
Netanyahu also shared "condolences to the families of those murdered" and "wishes for a full recovery to the injured."

UAE declares "full solidarity with Saudi Arabia" after deadly airport attack
The United Arab Emirates issued a statement Sunday reiterating its "full solidarity with Saudi Arabia" after the deadly attack on Riyadh's airport. The statement, released by the country's Ministry of Foreign Affairs, said the UAE supports "all measures" to protect security in the kingdom.
"The UAE expressed its sincere condolences and sympathy to the families of the victims, as well as its wishes for a speedy recovery for all the injured," the statement said.
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-trump-us-elections-saudi-arabia-yemen-houthis/#post-update-2e4f0756
Houthis issue new threat to Saudi airports and airspace, warn against travel
Yemen's Houthis have issued a new threat to Saudi Arabia's airspace since an attack on the kingdom's international airport in Riyadh killed 12 people and wounded hundreds, forcing authorities to stop operations.
Although the Iran-backed group hasn't claimed the strike, its military spokesperson threatened new attacks on the kingdom's airports and airspace, saying in a statement that they're "potential targets and sites for operations by our forces," CBS partner BBC News reported.
"The airports and airspace are exposed to targeting and constitute a theatre of operations for our forces," Yahya Saree, the Houthi spokesperson, wrote Sunday in a post on X. Passengers have been warned not to travel.
The holy cities of Mecca and Medina wouldn't be targeted, Saree said.
Saturday's attack, the deadliest on Saudi soil in more than a decade, was a sharp escalation in the Yemen war, where Riyadh backs the internationally-recognized government against the Houthis, who captured swaths of Red Sea coastline in a lightning offensive last month.
The strike came in a week when the group intensified its attacks on the Saudi capital's King Khalid International Airport, where three people were also killed on Thursday, as well as other key targets in the world's top crude oil exporter.
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-trump-us-elections-saudi-arabia-yemen-houthis/#post-update-9caa4d64
France on Sunday condemned the "heinous attack" by Yemen's Iran-backed Houthis on Riyadh's international airport a day earlier, stressing its "total solidarity" with Saudi Arabia.
France, which is already lending troops and military equipment to Saudi Arabia, "will continue to remain by its side" in order to defend "its territory and its infrastructure," the French Foreign Ministry said in a statement.
The ministry called on the Houthis to cease their attacks, echoing a previous statement issued by the French agency last week, in which it implored Houthi fighters to "immediately end their attacks and provocations, which destabilize the region further."
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-trump-us-elections-saudi-arabia-yemen-houthis/#post-update-d3f079e9
U.S. fighter jet strikes, disables commercial vessel in Gulf of Oman, Pentagon says
The U.S. military disabled a Panama-flagged commercial vessel in the Gulf of Oman Saturday by firing on it with a fighter jet, U.S. military's Central Command said.
According to CENTCOM, the fighter jet fired a "precision munition" at M/V Ocean Molica, also known as the "Arika Sun," that disabled the "ship's propulsion without harming the crew."
The ship had recently departed an Iranian port. Iran's ports have been under a U.S. blockade since a ceasefire between the two countries collapsed in July.
Four commercial vessels have been disabled by U.S. forces and 135 ships have been turned around since the blockade took effect on July 14, CENTCOM said.
During that same period, U.S. forces have also destroyed 10 tankers that are believed to be part of a "shadow fleet" that transport sanctioned Iranian oil and petroleum products, CENTCOM said.
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-trump-us-elections-saudi-arabia-yemen-houthis/#post-update-a9e19336
At least 12 killed, more than 300 injured in strike on Riyadh airport, Saudi officials say
At least 12 people were killed and 309 others wounded in a strike Saturday on Riyadh's King Khalid International Airport, Saudi officials said.
In a statement , the Saudi General Authority of Civil Aviation said an American was among the 12 victims. Four Saudi nationals, two Bangladeshi nationals, one Egyptian, one Jordanian, one Syrian, one Palestinian and one Sudanese national were also among those killed.
Another 309 people were wounded, the civil aviation authority said, an unknown number of whom were in critical condition.
The U.S. State Department has blamed the Iran-backed Houthi militant group for the strike, although the Houthis have not claimed responsibility for it.
"The United States strongly condemns the recent attacks on the Kingdom of Saudi Arabia by the terrorist Houthis, including today's strike on King Khalid International Airport in Riyadh that took numerous innocent lives, including an American citizen," Secretary of State Marco Rubio said in a social media post.
A violent conflict between a Saudi-backed coalition that supports the Yemeni government and the Houthis, who control a large swath of Yemen, has been building for weeks as the Houthis have expanded control of Yemen's Red Sea coastline and several islands in the Bab al-Mandab Strait, a critical waterway that has put further pressure on the movement of oil globally.
Earlier Saturday, President Trump said he was considering whether to have the U.S. join the fight against the Houthis.

The U.S. State Department on Saturday condemned what it called "Houthi terrorist attacks against civilian infrastructure and airports" in Saudi Arabia.
A State Department spokesperson told CBS News it is in close contact with its "Saudi partners."
The Houthis have not claimed responsibility for Saturday's strike on Riyadh's King Khalid International Airport, in which there were multiple injuries and fatalities, according to the State Department. The airport's operations have been shut down, according to Saudi Arabia's aviation authority.
" The United States stands firmly with the Kingdom of Saudi Arabia against Houthi terrorism," the spokesperson said.

U.S. "may" join Saudi strikes on Houthis, Trump says
President Trump left open the possibility the U.S. will join Saudi Arabia's battle against the Iran-backed Houthis on Saturday.
"We may. We're gonna look at it," Mr. Trump said when asked about the possibility as he left the White House for a campaign rally in Tennessee. "We just found out about the recent attack [on the airport]. So we'll make a decision&ndash; we move very quickly."
Mr. Trump was also asked about whether the Houthi strike on Riyadh's airport would change his previously announced decision not to launch any potential strikes on Iran until after the midterms.
Multiple people were injured in a strike on Riyadh's King Khalid International Airport on Saturday. The Houthis have not claimed responsibility for the attack, but Saudi Arabia's aviation authority said the strike was part of "continued attacks targeting civil aviation facilities in the Kingdom."
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-trump-us-elections-saudi-arabia-yemen-houthis/#post-update-233591f2
Multiple injuries in Riyadh airport attack, operations suspended
There have been multiple injuries at Riyadh's King Khalid International Airport, which has now shut down operations as officials assess the damage, according to the country's aviation authority.
"The attack resulted in a number of injuries, which are being dealt with," the General Authority of Civil Aviation said in a statement, the first official confirmation of an attack. "The authority explained that airport operations have been temporarily suspended to allow for the completion of damage assessments and verification of the safety of the facilities, in coordination with the relevant authorities."
There have been 324 flights into or out of King Khalid International Airport canceled on Saturday, according to FlightAware. Saudia airline, a pilot for which was killed in a Houthi strike on Thursday, has canceled the most flights (182) on Saturday.
The Iran-backed Houthis have gotten more bold in striking the airport, which is a major travel hub and hundreds of miles from where their power is located in Yemen. The militant organization has targeted the Riyadh airport several times this week, killing four people and injuring several more. Multiple people were also injured in a strike on Abha International Airport, in the southern part of Saudi Arabia.

U.S. "strongly" warns citizens to avoid Riyadh airport following attack

"U.S. government employees working in Saudi Arabia require special authorization to use the King Khalid International Airport in Riyadh. U.S. citizens should be aware of potential flight cancellations, airspace closures, and travel disruptions."
While the airport nor the Iran-backed Houthis have confirmed an attack, there were widespread reports of an explosion at the main airport in Saudi's capital and reports of injuries.
The Houthis have attacked the airport, which is hundreds of miles north of the Yemen border, several times this week. Four people have been killed in Houthi attacks on the airport, including three people on Thursday. One of those killed was a pilot for Saudia airline.

Western embassies in Saudi warn citizens away from Riyadh airport after strike
The Canadian, German and Spanish embassies in Saudi Arabia on Saturday warned their citizens to avoid Riyadh's international airport after reports it was attacked.
Spain's embassy, in a message on X, alerted nationals to a "new attack" by Yemen's Iran-backed Houthis on the airport, where witnesses earlier told AFP an evacuation was ordered.
A diplomatic source separately told AFP that the Houthis had hit the airport with a missile around 3 p.m. local time.
Canada's embassy said only that unspecified "ongoing military activity" was affecting Riyadh's international airport, while Germany's mission alluded only to "the current situation."
France's embassy on Friday urged French nationals to take "utmost care" in Saudi Arabia because of "strikes in recent days by the Houthis." It also warned French citizens not to record images of zones that were hit, nor share any online.
Saudi Arabia has been tightly controlling public information about Houthi attacks on its soil in recent weeks, leaving media and residents unclear as to the danger.
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-trump-us-elections-saudi-arabia-yemen-houthis/#post-update-f94de70e
Airport in Saudi capital has been attacked again, officials say
The airport in the Saudi capital was attacked again Saturday and was evacuated, two regional officials said, two days after three Saudi citizens including a pilot were killed there as the kingdom faces an extraordinary series of assaults in the new front in the Iran war.
One official said air traffic to the Riyadh airport had stopped. The officials spoke on condition of anonymity because they weren't authorized to speak to the media.
King Khalid International Airport has been targeted multiple times in the week since a Saudi-led coalition launched a major military offensive against Iran-backed Houthi rebels in neighboring Yemen. The Houthis claimed responsibility for the earlier attacks, including one Wednesday that killed a Sudanese national.
There was no immediate Houthi statement Saturday. The Saudi government-run Center for International Communication, which oversees foreign media in the kingdom, didn't immediately respond to a request for comment.
A Riyadh resident, Ahmed Sayed, told The Associated Press he heard at least two loud explosions that rocked the capital.
The airport issued a terse alert telling passengers to check their flight status with airlines before heading to the airport.

People were being evacuated from Riyadh's international airport on Saturday, witnesses told AFP, raising fears of a fresh attack after strikes claimed by Yemen's Houthis killed three people there this week.
Saudi authorities did not confirm any new attack or evacuation, but one witness reported hearing an explosion while waiting to board a flight, and at least one person was injured in an unspecified incident at the airport, a relative told AFP.
The relative said the person suffered injuries to their shoulders and arms from shattered glass and was being taken to hospital.
Two other witnesses confirmed people were being evacuated, but did not know what triggered it.
The airport was hit by deadly attacks claimed by Yemen's Houthis on Thursday, though the Iran-backed group did not announce any fresh strikes on the facility on Saturday.

Yemeni government forces gaining ground on Houthis near key waterway
Yemeni military sources told AFP on Saturday their forces were advancing against the Iran-backed Houthis in areas around the Bab el-Mandeb Strait but have not yet secured control of the waterway, after the government launched an operation to capture lost territory.
The Saudi-backed government last week announced a counteroffensive aimed at retaking all areas held by the Houthis, after the pro-Iran group seized Yemen's entire western coastline in a lightning offensive.
The Houthis confirmed government forces attacked in the Bab el-Mandeb area but they claimed to have repelled the assault.
"Saudi-led forces attempted to advance from the Ras al-Ara area in Lahj governorate towards the area south of Bab al-Mandab," Hizam al-Asad, a member of the Houthis' political bureau, told AFP.
"However, our forces repelled them outside the Bab el-Mandeb zone, inflicting heavy losses in personnel and equipment and forcing the survivors to retreat.
"Our forces maintain control over Bab el-Mandeb, Mayun Island, and the remaining areas along the country's western coast," he said.
Sources from the military of Yemen's internationally recognised government also said Mayun Island &mdash; located in the heart of the strait &mdash; remained in Houthi hands as fighting continued.

Putin relayed Iran's position on peace deal to Trump, Kremlin says
Russian President Vladimir Putin has told Donald Trump about Iran's position on a possible Mideast peace deal, the Kremlin said Saturday.
The Kremlin said Tehran's stance was relayed in a phone call held between the two leaders on Friday. It did not say how Trump responded, nor elaborate on Iran's position.
Diplomatic efforts to end the Iran conflict, which began with U.S.-Israeli strikes on Iran in February, have been at an impasse since the summer.
Putin met Iranian President Masoud Pezeshkian on the sidelines of a summit in Turkmenistan earlier this week, where the Russian leader expressed an interest in helping broker peace.
"In coordination with the Iranian president, Putin conveyed the Iranian side's views on a possible resolution to the conflict to Trump," Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov said Saturday, according to Russia's state RIA news agency.
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-trump-us-elections-saudi-arabia-yemen-houthis/#post-update-cc926d8d
Trump says Iran can either "give us everything or they don't exist any longer"
During a rally in upstate New York, President Trump said, "Iran will be very quick, one way or the other. Either they give us everything, or they don't exist any longer. They know that"
The president made the comments while criticizing the Nobel Peace Prize committee for not awarding him the prize &mdash; a frequent complaint by Mr. Trump. Calling the body "crooked," Mr. Trump attacked it for giving the prize to former International Criminal Court Judge Navanethem Pillay, whom the president called a "mystery person."
"This is an indelible stain on the country of Norway and those people that made such a disgraceful and embarrassing decision," he said. "The Nobel Peace Prize has been forever discredited by a handful of radical ideologues who are anti-democracy and really enemies of civilization."

Saudi-led coalition says it destroyed more than 100 Houthi targets
The Saudi-led coalition fighting Yemen's Houthis said Saturday it destroyed more than 100 military targets in a "large-scale operation" against the Iran-backed group.
"We are carrying out a large-scale operation targeting the personnel and capabilities of the Houthi terrorist militia," Turki al-Maliki, spokesperson for the Saudi coalition, wrote on X.
"We destroyed 136 military targets in areas controlled by the Houthi terrorist militia," he said in a separate post.
The Houthi's Almasirah TV channel reported that Saudi missiles targeted areas in the northwestern Yemeni governorate of Saada.
The conflict between the Houthis and Yemen's internationally recognized government &mdash; which Saudi Arabia supports &mdash; reignited in July, and has since spilled over into the neighboring kingdom.
In addition to seizing territory along the Red Sea coast in Yemen, the Houthis have attacked transport hubs, oil facilities and other civilian targets in the world's largest crude exporter with drones and missiles.
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-trump-us-elections-saudi-arabia-yemen-houthis/#post-update-1fdbb3d0
Trump says Russia will release millions of tons of diesel
President Trump said Russia has agreed to provide the world with millions of tons of diesel fuel over the next several months in a bid to decrease skyrocketing global prices.
In a Truth Social post, the president said he had just gotten off the phone with Russian President Vladimir Putin, who had agreed to release 300,000 tons of diesel immediately. He said another 500,000 tons of diesel would be released in November, followed by 1 million tons in December.
"Additionally, based on the condition of their Diesel Refineries, Russia will then deliver, within a short period of time, 3,000,000 Tons of Diesel Fuel," Mr. Trump continued.
The Treasury Department also announced Friday that it would temporarily waive sanctions on Russia's energy sector to "allow the supply of Russian diesel to the global market."
Mr. Trump had earlier Friday teased a "big announcement coming up on diesel" during remarks at a Columbus Day celebration.
Diesel prices have skyrocketed in recent months amid the war with Iran. The average price of a gallon of diesel is $6.28, according to AAA, which is up from $5.94 a month ago and $3.68 a year ago.
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-trump-us-elections-saudi-arabia-yemen-houthis/#post-update-0520e0f2
President Trump teased a "big announcement coming up on diesel" during remarks Friday at a Columbus Day celebration, but offered little information to speculate on what the announcement would be about.
Mr. Trump pointed out Energy Secretary Chris Wright and said he was "doing a great job."
"We have, I think, some big news on that diesel situation," the president said. "It's gonna &mdash; we're gonna be knocking the hell out of it. And remember, what have we done? We stopped Iran from having a nuclear weapon. That's big time. And the prices &mdash; the prices will come tumbling."
Diesel prices have skyrocketed in recent months amid the war with Iran. The average price of a gallon of diesel is $6.28, according to AAA , which is up from $5.94 a month ago and $3.68 a year ago.
Diesel had never previously crossed $6 a gallon until just before Labor Day weekend. The record, hit on Sept. 22, was $6.53 per gallon. Diesel is widely used in the trucking, agriculture and construction sectors, so it's a factor for nearly every type of good, including groceries.
The Trump administration has looked into several ways to lower diesel prices, most recently signing an executive order this week allowing the use of red-dyed diesel on highways. Red-dyed diesel is exempt from the federal tax of 24.4 cents a gallon that applies to regular diesel.

Sri Lanka's government says it has no immediate plans to help 19 Iranian ships that have been anchored in the international waters near the island nation facing food, water and fuel shortages.
Foreign Ministry spokesman Thushara Rodrigo said Sri Lanka will put its national interest first before considering any help, and the companies that supply ships are aware of possible secondary sanctions from the United States.
He said, however, there hasn't been any communication from the U.S. warning of repercussions should Sri Lanka decide to help.

The U.K., France, Germany, Australia and Canada have advised their citizens against travel to parts of Saudi Arabia following a wave of Houthi attacks, including one that killed three civilians at Riyadh's King Khalid International Airport on Thursday.
The U.K. Foreign Office now advises against all but essential travel to the Riyadh region, Najran, Jizan and Aseer provinces, Taif city and Taif International Airport, and against all travel within six miles of the Yemeni border.
France says travel to Najran, Abha, Khamis Muhsait and Djizan in the south of the kingdom " remains strongly discouraged ," and Germany , Australia and Canada have all advised against all travel to the country.

U.S. government employees also require "special authorization for travel to the provinces of Jizan, Asir, Najran, and the cities of Qatif, Yanbu, and Taif."

Pakistan has a "substantial number of forces" deployed in Saudi Arabia in a defensive capacity, Pakistan's foreign ministry spokesman said Friday.

USS Lincoln returns home to San Diego for first time in over 10 months
The USS Abraham Lincoln and its more than 4,500 sailors returned home to San Diego on Thursday for the first time in 321 days, a jubilant homecoming after a long deployment that included months in the Middle East.
Braving the heat, hundreds of family members and friends waited for hours in the sun to greet their loved ones. As the ship pulled into the harbor, a roar of cheers erupted from a pier. Sailors stood at attention on the deck for the aircraft carrier's long approach, and after they were released, smiles and waves broke out as sailors took out their phones to call people on the pier.
In one such reunion, McKenzie Adkins held her 10-week-old son Declan as she waved to her husband, Alexander, on the ship's deck, before the new father met his son for the first time.
The Lincoln carrier strike group had been deployed for more than 10 months , including more than 250 uninterrupted days at sea this year. Much of that time was spent in the Middle East, where the aircraft carrier and its escort ships supported the U.S. war against Iran. It left the region in late August.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Fox News senior White House correspondent Peter Doocy has the latest on the world's energy crisis and the G7 nations' big announcement on 'Special Report.'
Heating season is here, and the Iran war is getting the blame for higher energy prices . The war explains the timing. It doesn't explain the vulnerability.
America produces enormous amounts of oil and gas. Why do prices jump when the Persian Gulf flares? Because oil trades on a world market. A crisis anywhere moves prices everywhere.
But the real problem is policies at the state level that have kept us from truly unleashing the bounty of our domestic energy supply.
CHEVRON CEO WARNS IRAN CONFLICT HAS CREATED 'VERY REAL' THREAT TO GLOBAL OIL SUPPLIES AS GAS PRICES CLIMB
It’s been well documented that we have a refinery shortage. The country has 130 operable refineries, down from 301 in 1982, and no major new refinery has been built in the U.S. since 1976. Here in the Northeast, when we lost the Philadelphia Energy Solutions plant, we lost about 335,000 barrels a day.
A part of the Trans Alaska Pipeline System is seen on Sept. 17, 2019, in Fairbanks, Alaska. The 800-mile-long pipeline carries oil from Prudhoe Bay to Valdez. (Photo by Joe Raedle/Getty Images)
In my home state of New York , like so many other states, we also have a self-imposed supply problem. Natural gas moves through pipelines, and New York has blocked those pipelines. Pennsylvania, right next door, sits on some of the richest gas fields in the country. The Constitution Pipeline, if constructed, would carry that natural gas 124 miles, with 99 miles of new right-of-way in New York.
AMERICAN BUSINESSES AREN’T ALLOWED TO BUILD. IT’S TIME CONGRESS HELPED THEM
The fight to build this much-needed pipeline is now 10 years old. In April 2016, the state denied the water permit. Courts upheld it. The company tried again in 2025, then withdrew in November after three notices that its application was incomplete.
And the problem runs deeper than one pipeline. Ill-conceived climate laws set sweeping emissions targets, and the plan to reach them is thin if not unattainable. Take New York City's Local Law 97. It fines large buildings that exceed emissions limits, and the limits tighten sharply in 2030. Owners must pay for costly retrofits or pay penalties. Either way, the bill lands on tenants, co-op owners and small businesses.
THE REAL REASON YOUR ELECTRIC BILL IS SOARING THIS SUMMER WILL SURPRISE YOU
Democrats in too many state capitals seem determined to choke off other reliable and cost-effective energy sources. Nuclear reactors and coal plants have closed down, both of which supply much-needed energy to citizens, especially low-income families.
Meanwhile, the utility bills keep climbing , filling your gas tank becomes more painful, and truckers and shippers face punishing prices for diesel, which, of course, trickles down to raise the cost of everything from lumber to lunch meat.
SENATORS PRESS PRESIDENT TRUMP TO RELEASE HOME HEATING OIL RESERVES AMID SOARING PRICES
I’ve spent over 20 years in the energy and utility industry. Energy is not abstract. It is pipe, wire, steel and supply. Limit supply while demand holds, and prices rise. That is arithmetic, not ideology.
And winter makes it more brutal. On the coldest days, when pipes run full, utilities scramble for alternatives and alternatives cost more. Families on fixed incomes can't opt out. They lower the thermostat and pull on another sweater.
TRUMP ADMIN MOVES TO GUT BIDEN'S SIGNATURE ENVIRONMENTAL RULES
Here is what should bother everyone, whatever their politics. A state that won't produce or import its own fuel doesn't become independent. It becomes a customer. It bids against the world for supply and accepts whatever price is asked, including prices set by wars it can't influence.
The fight to build this much-needed pipeline is now 10 years old. In April 2016, the state denied the water permit. Courts upheld it. The company tried again in 2025, then withdrew in November after three notices that its application was incomplete.
What's the solution? Approve pipeline projects with capacity that bring in domestic gas. Keep reliable power plants running until real replacements are built and tested. Approve the construction of new refineries. Be honest about what climate mandates cost and phase them in at a pace working families can afford. Judge success by lower bills and steady supply, not ribbon-cuttings.
And most importantly: Vote for candidates this November who are dedicated to American energy independence , supporting sensible and robust expansion of both production and refinement of our own bountiful energy supply.
Dave Bovee spent 20 years in the energy and utility industry. His book, "The Four G Revolution: The Fight to Save God, Gas, Guns, and Glory," was published by RealClear Publishing on October 6.
